【译】——要紧的。有一点我要说，是我们还没有触及的，那就是极端的权力集中。过去 12 个月里我们已经看到，行政分支（executive branch）对前沿 AI 公司如何运营的介入深得多了，有时方式相当没有原则，就是在施展它们的权力和控制，基本上是在摆布这些 AI 公司。我非常担心，如果我们真的走向智能爆炸，这条轨迹会是什么样子——如果行政分支里有人只想把所有那些强大的技术拿过来，用于自己的目的，不受国会任何监督，也不受更广大公众的任何监督。所以这是一整个我们没机会深入的问题，但我要说，它确实给某些做法增加了一点微妙之处，比如那些锁定智能爆炸、确保 AI 不会接管的做法，会导致一种新的问题，也就是行政分支过度、过度集中的权力。所以我认为，在我们如何拿捏平衡这件事上，必须非常、非常、非常小心。但我要说，我不认为什么都不做是可以接受的。我确实认为我们需要某种——某种监管介入，让我们不只是冲向一场智能爆炸。我们只是需要把它设计得谨慎。Tom，能请你来太好了，希望我们能再请你来。是啊，非常有趣。多谢各位。谢谢，Tom。再见。好极了，我们继续。下一位嘉宾是——

[4800s - 4920s]

Mackenzie Arnold. Mackenzie Arnold is the managing director of U.S. law and policy at the Institute for Law & AI. The institute conducts legal research and advises organizations working on how artificial intelligence should be governed. His own research focuses on administrative law, how government agencies make decisions, and liability. During 2026, he has co-authored on when AI security incidents must be reported, what companies should disclose about their internal use of powerful models, and how Congress can use defense legislation to shape AI oversight. In October, he and colleagues proposed changes to California's incident reporting provisions. He was also a reviewer of Carnegie's November report on implementing frontier AI policy. Before joining the institute, McKenzie clerked for Judge Joseph A. Greenway Jr. on the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit and worked in public health law at a New York nonprofit. He graduated from Harvard Law School and studied political science at Boston College. Before law school, he completed a Fulbright grant in Ourense, Spain. McKenzie, welcome to the show. Hey folks, thanks for having me. Great to have you. Great to meet you. I've been following you online for some time. Obviously, maybe start the same way we started with Tom, which is there is a lot going on. Proposals are flying around, you know, with increasing volume. What is standing out to you right now as the you know the most important thing that your gaze is being drawn toward? Yeah, maybe this is just the the lawyer in me, but this is the moment that everyone kind of waits for. People are actually writing things right now, right? And and one of my big takeaways over the last couple of years, and especially this summer, right, is that conversation gets so much smarter when we have specific things to argue over, right? When we started seeing actual cyber incidents and autonomous incidents, it was.

【译】——Mackenzie Arnold。Mackenzie Arnold 是法律与 AI 研究所（Institute for Law and AI）美国法律与政策董事总经理。该研究所从事法律研究，并为致力于研究应如何治理人工智能的机构提供咨询。他自己的研究重点是行政法——政府机构如何做决策——以及责任问题。2026 年期间，他合著了关于 AI 安全事件何时必须上报、公司应就其内部使用强大模型披露什么，以及国会如何利用国防立法来塑造 AI 监督的文章。10 月，他和同事对加州的事件报告条款提出了修改建议。他还是卡内基 11 月关于落实前沿 AI 政策报告的审阅人。加入研究所之前，Mackenzie 曾为美国第三巡回上诉法院的 Joseph A. Greenaway Jr. 法官担任法官助理，并在纽约一家非营利组织从事公共卫生法工作。他毕业于哈佛法学院，在波士顿学院学习政治学。读法学院之前，他在西班牙奥伦塞完成了富布赖特项目。Mackenzie，欢迎来参加节目。嘿，各位，谢谢邀请我。很高兴请到你。很高兴认识你。我在网上关注你有一阵子了。显然，也许我们可以像和 Tom 开始时那样开始：现在发生的事情很多，提案满天飞，音量越来越大。此刻有什么东西突显出来，是你认为最重要、最吸引你目光的？是啊，也许这只是我身上的律师劲儿，但这就是所有人都在等的时刻。人们现在真的在动笔写东西了，对吧？过去几年、尤其是这个夏天，我的一个大体会是：当我们有了具体可以争的东西时，对话会变得聪明得多，对吧？当我们开始看到真实的网络安全事件和自主性事件时，那——

[4920s - 5040s]

Great! It's one of the few times I've ever been happy with with Twitter. Is seeing a bunch of people argue over those incidents and what we can learn from it. And the same thing's happening on a bunch of these policy proposals right now. So, sort of old news, but continuing on, there are a bunch of proposals around auditing third party evaluators, incident reporting, sort of all these transparency requirements that for a long time stayed in this kind of pre baked form. Everything was voluntary. All the thresholds were super high. Everyone kind of working on this knew that that was not going to be where things were going to end up. But there wasn't the political will or interest to sort of solve those problems. And luckily, our institute and a lot of others had a lot of good thinking, sort of locked and loaded, ready to go there. And a lot of those laws are being edited very quickly and improved. And really, the name of the game on all of this is that on the federal level, you have no idea which proposal is actually going to win the day. And so, really, you want to see these improvements proliferate across a bunch of different proposals. Then there are the sort of new frontier type of things where we've been seeing a lot of proposals around both understanding and insight into the rate of AI R and D inside of these companies, and it's sort of the outer edge proposals to restrict, constrain, put thresholds on that AI R and D if it starts to become recursive. So, I think that's an area where I hope in a couple of months our state of knowledge will be much more similar to where it is on the transparency proposals. This is sort of the first time where people are taking it seriously enough and drafting. And so, hopefully, we're going to work through a bunch of these hard questions. But maybe you have some good questions to work through those today. Maybe just to start us off, could you could you define some of these things like artificial intelligence, super intelligence, recursive, you know, self improvement? What are the definitions that policymakers are evolving towards on these on these items? Yeah, starting at the most basic on defining AI itself, right? Oftentimes when we talk with policymakers, they think that there's one.

【译】——太棒了！那是我少数几次对 Twitter 感到高兴的时候之一——看到一群人围着那些事件争论，讨论我们能从中学到什么。而现在在一堆政策提案上也在发生同样的事。所以算是旧闻了，但接着往下说：围绕审计、第三方评估、事件报告，也就是所有这些透明度要求，有一堆提案，它们长期以来都停留在某种预制好的形式里。一切都是自愿的，所有门槛都超级高。每个做这一行的人都知道事情最终不会停在那种状态，但当时没有解决这些问题的政治意愿或兴趣。幸运的是，我们研究所和很多其他机构早就有了很多好的思考，上膛待发，准备好了。而其中很多法律正在被非常迅速地修改和改进。而这一切的关键在于：在联邦层面，你根本不知道哪个提案最终会胜出。所以你真正想看到的是这些改进在一堆不同的提案里扩散开来。然后还有那些新前沿类的东西，我们看到很多提案围绕着了解和洞察这些公司内部 AI 研发的速度，还有一些最外围的提案，主张在 AI 研发开始变得具有递归性时对其加以限制、约束、设置门槛。所以我认为这是一个我希望几个月后我们的知识状态能接近透明度提案那个水平的领域。这算是第一次人们足够认真地对待它并开始起草。所以希望我们会解决掉一堆这样的难题。不过也许你今天有一些好问题来把它们过一遍。也许我们可以这样开头：你能不能定义一下这些东西，比如人工智能、超级智能、递归的——自我改进？在这些项目上，政策制定者们正趋向于什么样的定义？好，从最基础的、定义 AI 本身开始，对吧？我们和政策制定者交谈时，他们常常以为存在某一个——

[5040s - 5160s]

Sort of holy grail definition that's out there in the ether, right? And really, when you're drafting laws, that that's not the case, right? Your purpose, the purpose of definitions, really, is to constrain the powers of the government or to constrain where you're actually regulating. And so, if you have a really hard or difficult piece of regulation, if especially on the sort of ex ante side, if you're requiring a lot of proactive steps by a company, you might want to restrict that. You might want to have a less expansive category of what's actually covered. On the other hand, if you have something like incident reporting, where the very fact that you have to report means that something bad happened, right? You might not have to have as as restrictive of a definition, right? You might actually be okay with a broader definition of AI because where the work is being done is the definition of the bad thing that happened, right? So you don't care as much where it's coming from, so long as it as it meets that other criteria. On the RSI AI R and D side, I think it's a similar thing, right? There is no sort of definition out in the ether that's the exact right one. You might want to have slightly different categories of these definitions. So, so one of the key things is you really care about recursiveness, right? AI R and D in and of itself, right? AI improving other AIs. If it if there isn't a sort of enough of a reward to that, if the if the returns are diminishing, you don't. As returns to returns to the AI or to the the rate of progress in in how much the AI is improving, right? What what people are really worried about is almost akin to maybe think of perpetual motion or something. What what is the point that you reach at which the the returns to the rate of improvement are so high that you stop needing to give additional inputs? Maybe you still need material inputs, but but sort of the pace continues to accelerate with a lot without a lot of additional intervention. So long as you stay under that threshold, right? That's a lot more manageable of a process. It's it's a lot easier to imagine humans maintaining understanding, maintaining control in that process. And so one version of this.

【译】——某种放之四海而皆准的圣杯式定义，悬在半空中，对吧？而在起草法律的时候，实际情况并非如此，对吧？定义的目的，其实是要约束政府的权力，或者约束你实际监管的范围。所以，如果你有一项非常艰难、非常苛刻的监管，尤其是在事前（ex ante）那一面，要求公司采取大量主动措施，你可能就会想限制它，可能想让实际覆盖的范围别那么宽。另一方面，如果是像事件报告这样的东西，需要上报这件事本身就意味着有坏事发生了，对吧？那你的定义就不必那么严格，对吧？你也许可以接受一个更宽泛的 AI 定义，因为真正起作用的是对「发生了什么坏事」的定义，对吧？所以你不太在意它来自哪里，只要它满足那个其他标准就行。在递归自我改进（RSI）、AI 研发这一边，我觉得是类似的道理，对吧？半空中并不存在某个恰好正确的定义。你可能想要对这些定义做稍微不同的分类。所以关键之一是你真的很在意递归性，对吧？AI 研发本身，AI 改进其他 AI。如果这没有足够的回报，如果回报是递减的，你就——随着——随着对 AI 的回报，或者说 AI 改进速度的——递减，对吧？人们真正担心的东西几乎有点像永动机之类的东西。到了哪个点，改进速度的回报会高到让你不再需要投入额外输入？也许你仍然需要物质投入，但节奏会在不需要大量额外干预的情况下持续加速。只要你还停在那个门槛之下，对吧？那个过程就可控得多，很容易想象人类在这种过程中保持理解、保持控制。所以这个问题的一个版本——

[5160s - 5280s]

Definition that people are really trying to operationalize is this: What's the point of no return? What's the point at which you lose comprehension of the process? What's the what's the point at which this becomes recursive and continues to sort of self-perpetuate? Those are the definitions I think that will be most important for these hard cap type proposals or interventions. On the other hand, some of the proposals, like in the California report, which I know each of you have reviewed, are just about giving an understanding to the government and the public about the rate of this progress internally. And so that that's another one where it might be less important to set such a high thresholders to get the the definition exactly right, because really what you want is an impressionistic sense of how worried should we be? What's the sense inside of these companies? Does it feel like it's accelerating over time? Does it feel like they're hitting snags and bottlenecks? And that's really the information you want on the policy end, right? It's in some ways it's a a nice overlap in the interest of people who are most worried and least worried about the tech. You just kind of want to adjudicate on the facts. If if it's if it's really concerning, you want to know. If it's not concerning, you want to know as well. So so do you think you're going to see the informational and transparency requirements pass first rather than the restrictions, or do you think it's going to be packaged up, you know, totally and like it's going to be, you know, we're gonna we're gonna end up with the restrictions on top of the you know transparency, you know, in one go at this point. Yeah, I'm really not sure. I mean, even right trying to predict what would be possible at the end of this year, if you'd asked me in April of last year or something, my predictions would have been horribly off, or at least they would have been in this, you know, contingent on certain things in the world happening. You know, there will be interventions and contingent on it taking another year to start to see some of these results. Right? It would go a different direction, and so I think we're in a similar spot now where it's really hard to predict. I do think it is it's totally feasible to get both of those at the same time, both the informational part and the restriction part. And I guess from my narrow perspective, again.

【译】——人们真正试图把它操作化的定义是这样的：不可返回点（point of no return）在哪里？你在哪个点上失去对过程的理解？在哪个点上它变得具有递归性，并开始自我延续？我认为对于这些硬上限（hard cap）类提案或干预措施来说，这些才是最重要的定义。另一方面，有些提案，比如加州报告里的——我知道你们两位都审阅过——只是为了让政府和公众了解这种进展在公司内部的速度。所以那是另一类，在那里设高门槛、把定义搞得精确无误可能没那么重要，因为你真正想要的是一种印象式的感觉：我们该有多担心？这些公司内部的感觉是什么？感觉它在随时间加速吗？感觉他们在卡壳、碰壁吗？而在政策层面，这才是你真正想要的信息，对吧？在某种程度上，这是对这项技术最担心的人和最不担心的人的利益的一次漂亮重合——你就是想根据事实来裁决。如果真的很令人担忧，你想知道；如果不令人担忧，你也想知道。那么你认为信息与透明度要求会先通过、限制措施后通过，还是你认为会被打包在一起，就是说最终限制措施会叠在透明度之上，在现阶段一步到位？是啊，我真的不确定。我是说，光是预测今年年底什么是可能的——如果你在去年 4 月左右问我，我的预测会离谱地错，或者至少那些预测是以世界上某些事情的发生为前提的。会有干预措施，并以「再过一年才开始看到其中一些结果」为前提，对吧？方向就会不同。所以我认为我们现在处在类似的境地，真的很难预测。我确实认为同时得到这两样东西是完全可行的，既有信息部分，也有限制部分。而我猜，从我这狭窄的视角出发，再一次——

[5280s - 5400s]

Maybe this is overly lawyer brand. I just want people to be writing both of these proposals in parallel, because one mistake would be to just focus on the transparency side of things and just wait for the window to open or the day to come to figure out the other parts. When in fact, if you're really talking about caps or restrictions, it's going to take a lot of arguing and red teaming for a very long time to get a proposal that is actually worthwhile. And if anything, those who are least concerned about the progress of the tech or most worried about government overreach should be the most invested in trying to work those proposals out now, because it is going to be a really hard process to figure out what does judicial review look like. How do you escape under the thumb of the government if there is in fact a restriction on the internal AI R and D that you're doing? And so I think you know full speed ahead. People work these out. I'm not sure when the window will open and sort of what order will get the proposals. How do you feel about states' roles to date and in the short term? I mean, I've always been a big laboratories of democracy guy. Timelines obviously might be a little short for that feedback cycle to close. My impulse is still to say, like, let the states do their thing. You know, there it can't hurt. What's your attitude on the relationship between the federal government and the states? Yeah, I also tend to think the states have a lot to contribute here, and I'm more worried at the margins about restricting their ability to act than I am about them overacting. One thing on this idea of sort of laboratories of democracy, right? You talk to some commentators, especially in the law, and a lot of them are very pessimistic about the idea of laboratories of democracy, and that's because they focus on this. Well, actually, what you often get is if one state adopts it, the other ones copy it, or even if you get differing proposals, they don't do some sort of analysis that's careful of the costs and benefits of each proposal. And so this idea that you learn a lot through what's passed.

【译】也许这有点过于律师思维了。我只是希望人们同时起草这两类提案，因为一个错误的做法是只盯着透明度这一面，然后坐等窗口打开、坐等那一天到来，再去琢磨其他部分。而事实上，如果你真要谈上限或限制，那需要非常长时间的反复争论和红队测试（red teaming），才能拿出一份真正有价值的提案。甚至可以说，那些最不担心技术进展、或者最担心政府越权的人，恰恰最应该投入到现在就把这些提案琢磨清楚，因为搞清司法审查（judicial review）究竟长什么样，会是一个非常艰难的过程。如果对你正在做的内部 AI 研发真的施加了限制，你要怎么从政府的掌控下脱身？所以我认为，全速前进，大家把这些琢磨出来。我不确定窗口什么时候会打开，也不确定会以什么顺序拿到这些提案。到目前为止以及短期内，你怎么看各州扮演的角色？这个嘛，我一直是个坚定的「民主实验室」（laboratories of democracy）派。时间线显然可能有点短，等不及那个反馈循环闭合。我的直觉仍然是，让各州自己去搞。这不会有坏处。你对联邦政府与各州之间的关系是什么态度？是的，我也倾向于认为各州在这里能贡献很多，而且在边际上，我更担心的是限制它们行动的能力，而不是它们行动过头。关于「民主实验室」这个想法，有一点值得说，对吧？你去和一些评论者聊聊，尤其是法学界的，很多人对「民主实验室」的想法非常悲观，原因在于他们盯着这一点：实际上，你经常看到的是，一个州通过了，其他州就照抄；或者即便提案各不相同，它们也不会对每个提案的成本和收益做什么仔细的分析。所以那种「通过立法就能学到很多东西」的想法……

Comparison or something historically has often not really checked out. It's lucky in many ways that California happens to be the center of of this industry and also you know just a large you know population and economic center. It makes you know you could imagine there being more of these sort of commerce clauses or interfering with other states' worries if you had a larger contrast. If it in fact was you know California trying to interfere in some industry in based somewhere else, they in fact have a pretty legitimate interest in things going on in their own borders. 【译】……通过什么立法的比较之类的，历史上往往真的经不起检验。不过我认为「民主实验室」还有另一个版本，要强得多，那就是：在一个州内部对法案的争论、修改过程，会让后来的提案聪明得多。所以几年前我们所有人都为加州参议院第 1047 号法案（SB 1047）争过一轮，搞清了它哪里行、哪里不行，从那以后的所有提案都因为这件事好了很多。同样的事现在也在发生。加州在 IVOs 上动手早，伊利诺伊州在审计上动手早，这推动了大量工作，让我们现在处理这个问题时处于更有利的位置。以至于到了今年夏天之后，当你突然看到一批国会议员在网上说「我们现在都想对 AI 采取行动」时，他们不必只能依赖外面某份含糊的白皮书。有鉴于此，我确实认为各州在这里积极主动是非常好的事。加州恰好是这个产业的中心，同时又是一个人口和经济大州，这在很多方面是幸运的。如果反差更大，你可以想象会出现更多这类商业条款（commerce clause）问题，或者「干预其他州」的担忧。而如果是加州想干预某个设在别处的产业，他们对自己州界内发生的事情其实有着相当正当的利益。我们来看看 10 月的那个。我想你发布了一份提案，要求人类必须理解 AI 生成的研究，并把这当作那类进展能否继续推进的一种标志。你能多解释一下这个概念吗？好的，你指的也许是 Gillian Hadfield 和我的同事 Bahrad Sokhansanj 写的那篇论文，我想那篇论文还有第三作者。没给人家署名，在此致歉。这里的想法是围绕「理解审计」（comprehension audits）展开的。传统上……

[5520s - 5640s]

A lot, or at least so far, a lot of the auditing and third-party evaluator proposals have been more about incidents or model behavior, right? These these individual observation points, or they've been about general safety practices. Are you communicating things internally when you realize that something's up, and is someone going to act on it? A new category, and this is what I take the paper to be really be setting out, is you also might want to have a third party say, "Do we know what the heck is going on with the internal AI R and D within the company?" That seems particularly compelling if you expect that that's going to be a very squishy, hard to define category. That there might be really strong incentives for the companies to be secretive about their own internal AI R and D processes. That, but you know, on sort of like both hands, sometimes they might have incentives to exaggerate how good it is when when it sounds impressive, and it you know sort of relates to their own investment. Other times they maybe they may have reasons to underplay it, especially if they expect that there's going to be strong government intervention. And so, wouldn't it be useful if we're already sort of inserting these third parties as sort of neutral arbiters to also have them observing that process? And I think that you can have that work. And and sort of what what the paper is trying to outline is how can you have that work given that this is a very hard thing to answer, right? It's a very hard thing to quantify. How concerned should we be? What is the shape of of internal progress? And what they settle on is let's focus on how much the workers who are sort of you know administering these processes understand what's going on. If you have made one improvement in your AI system before you go to the next one, can you explain what the heck happened? Can you can you explain why it has functioned? Can you explain how much of a you know which of sort of like all the spiky vectors of capabilities which have gotten better and which ones haven't? I think there's a lot more work to be done to actually instrumentalize this. I I don't mean to sort of hand wave away the fact that this is a squishy category, and I do think that they're getting at.

【译】到目前为止，很多审计和第三方评估者（third-party evaluators）提案更多是关于事件或模型行为的，对吧？也就是这些一个个孤立的观察点；或者是关于一般性的安全实践——当你发现不对劲时，内部有没有沟通？会不会有人据此行动？一个新的类别——我认为这正是那篇论文真正想阐述的——是你可能还想让第三方来问一句：「我们到底知不知道这家公司内部的 AI 研发在发生什么？」如果你预期这将是一个非常模糊、难以界定的类别，这一点就显得尤其有说服力。公司可能有非常强的动机对自己的内部 AI 研发流程保密。但反过来，有时当它听起来很亮眼的时候，他们也可能有动机夸大它有多好，因为这关系到他们自己的融资。另一些时候，他们可能有理由低调处理，尤其是当他们预期会有强力政府干预的时候。所以，既然我们已经在把这些第三方作为中立仲裁者安插进去，让他们也顺便观察这个过程，岂不是很有用？我认为这是可以做到的。那篇论文试图勾勒的正是：鉴于这是个非常难回答的问题，怎样才能做到这一点。这很难量化。我们该有多担心？内部进展的形状是什么样的？他们最终的落点是：让我们聚焦于管理这些流程的员工在多大程度上理解正在发生什么。你在进入下一步改进之前，对 AI 系统做了一项改进，你能解释到底发生了什么吗？你能解释它为什么有效吗？在能力那许多参差不齐的维度里，你能解释哪些变强了、哪些没有吗？我认为要真正把它操作化，还有很多工作要做。我不是想敷衍过去「这是个模糊类别」的事实，我也确实认为他们触及了……

[5640s - 5760s]

One of these core concerns or considerations, which is we may not on the outside have a good sense of how concerned we should be or what the rate of progress is, and if there's any way to sort of quantify that or any way to even explain it qualitatively to the outside world, it would put us in a better spot. I think one of the I think one of the confusions that I think AI researchers have is that actually they don't know anything. It's actually it's actually the exact opposite. They don't they don't they're most AI researchers will tell you they almost know nothing. That we are we are very early in like understanding how neural networks work. We have some very early like interpretability and other very early in the design stage, very early in all of these things. And that one this is one of the concerns. This is the one of the concerns is that the capability is coming, you know, way way before the understanding. So I think like if you required a comprehension audit, I think we'd have to probably start stop at maybe GPT-2 probably like probably six years ago. That's that's about where we are right now. I think that's about the size of the models that I think we are able to interpret with with some degree of of certainty. So I think I think one of the one of the key confusions is how would the legal system deal with the fact that these guys have no idea what they're doing and and and also as some other people have pointed out that is true of biology as well. Like most of pharma is also completely unknown. You kind of do the that's why we experiment we because we are unable to design exactly. So we experiment and then we find out these things and we we experiment on humans too. We do human testing, right? So we do human phase one, phase two, phase three trials before we do pharma. So I think one of the one of the confusions is it's it's actually exactly the opposite. We know nothing. Like we know nothing. That's that's the baseline, right? So yeah, that's and that generalizes beyond this field, right? Like one of the marvels of modern sort of scientific knowledge is that oftentimes we can tell that a thing is.

【译】……这些核心担忧或考量之一：我们站在外面，可能并不清楚该有多担心、进展速率是多少；如果有什么办法把它量化，或者哪怕只是定性地向外界的解释清楚，都会让我们处于更有利的位置。我觉得有一个困惑，就是 AI 研究者其实什么都不知道。事实恰恰相反。他们不——大多数 AI 研究者会告诉你，他们几乎一无所知。在对神经网络如何工作的理解上，我们还非常早。我们有一些很早期的可解释性（interpretability）研究，在设计阶段也很早，所有这些方面都很早。而这就是担忧之一，担忧之一就是能力来得远远早于理解。所以我觉得，如果你要求做理解审计，我们可能大概得停在 GPT-2，大概大概六年前。那大概就是我们现在所处的位置。我想那大概就是我们能够带着一定确定性去解释的模型的规模。所以我觉得一个关键的困惑是：法律体系该如何处理「这些人根本不知道自己在干什么」这个事实——而且像其他人指出的那样，生物学也是如此。大多数药物的情况也完全未知。你只能——所以我们才要做实验，因为我们无法精确地设计。我们做实验，然后发现这些东西，我们也拿人做实验。我们做人体试验，对吧？药物上市前我们要做人体一期、二期、三期临床试验。所以我觉得其中一个困惑在于，事实恰恰相反。我们什么都不知道。我们真的什么都不知道。这就是基线，对吧？而且这一点不只适用于这个领域。现代科学知识的奇迹之一就是，我们常常能判断一件事是……

【译】……是真的，却不知道为什么，对吧？在非常多的领域里，我们的测量和观察能力已经变得非常强，但我们真正解释机制的能力呢？比如现在人们谈论 AI 正在解决的那些重大数学问题，就是这类例子之一：有一个现象，我们完全不知道怎么解释，我们也许有粗略的近似或者猜测，但要真正做到「理解」其实是非常难的任务。所以你说得完全对，如果标准是你需要对这东西有某种全知式的深刻理解，那门槛就太高了，我们其实现在就已经做不到了。我想很多埋头做这项技术的人会觉得这非常反直觉，甚至觉得「我们可以在这里拥有某种程度的理解」这种说法近乎荒谬。我认为法律在很多其他情境下已经想出了怎么处理这种事。想想侵权法（tort law），就是个好例子。在世上非常复杂的情况下，要弄清什么是合理行为标准（reasonable conduct）真的很难，对吧？要避免某种伤害，你得做多少培训？你得反复警告你的员工多少次？这里面就是有权衡的。有些东西对你的生意非常有用，但事实上错误率或者对人的风险率会略高一点。中间还有一片巨大的灰色地带：什么是最合理的，什么是比较合理的，什么是没那么合理的。但法律最后往往归结为：我们不需要解决所有这些问题，因为我们真正要找的是这个分布边缘的离群值。在这些情况下，你看起来是否极其不合理？你是否忽视了什么？你的行为方式是否与同侪完全格格不入？所以也许在某种认知的意义上，可以有类似的映射：是的，我们总是处于无知或困惑的状态，但总有些时候，你早上醒来看着你的工作会说：不，这感觉在性质上不一样了。这是一种……

【译】……还没准备好采取政策行动。要切分这个问题有好几种方式。我想先说一点：政治上的奥弗顿窗口其实是颠倒混乱、让人困惑的。我合作的一些倡导人士有时会被我自己的观点搞糊涂，因为我的观点往往是「这项政策干预对各种不同的世界观都站得住脚，所以人们不该太严厉地反对它」。说实话，这与通常左右政治算计的东西完全是两个问题，对吧？你常常会看到一些实际上很激进、并不被广泛认同的提案反而更受欢迎，因为它们更能调动情绪。所以如果我们在一些处于边缘的那类提案上先看到行动，我一点也不会惊讶——比如把递归自我改进（RSI）作为一个具体议题来处理，我们可能会看到这事先于其他事情落地，原因可能只是它创造了更多的激活能，感觉更重要，而且在很多方面对政策制定者来说确实非常重要。所以我想在一开始就说：如果你只问「我们最理解哪些东西」或者「哪些争议最小」之类的问题，其实很难预测政治领域会发生什么。就实际的理解而言，我认为简单的基本盘是事件透明度、审计等等。你永远不可能消除所有不确定性，但有很多历史先例，有很多著述，多年来有过很多打磨。这些看起来即便不是不用脑子的，也是相对低风险、理解相对充分的话题。你提到的处于外围的、我会称之为下一个前沿但一直很难推理的东西，是思考一个 AI 广泛扩散的世界、多智能体系统（multi-agent systems）。

A version of things where AI is widely deployed in the economy, and this has sort of you know implications up and down the stack. You know that's a really different world. Much of the history of the law is a history of humans figuring out how to deal with uncooperative or unpredictable agents, right? Or to to limit risk, or to figure out how how is it that you know we want it to be predictable? When I make a deal with your company that we will both cooperate with each other, that will follow through. That is, there's a whole another degree of complication when you start to add in AI, and I think that that is the sort of order of magnitudive intervention that we should be thinking about. That when when this happened amongst humans, or you know when we when we created you know corporations and other things, this took creating whole new bodies of law. It was not the sort of tinkering at the edges sort of thing. It's much more akin to creating antitrust law or intellectual property or all these things that we kind of forget. You know they didn't exist. They didn't just emerge. You know from from the ether back in the day. They were things that you know emerged in specific political moments when we had to deal with things. And AI is bringing us back to that point. And for many of us, we have never lived in a time in our own lifetimes when we were talking about you know founding or creating whole new bodies of law to deal deal with problems. And so that's I guess the outer edge. I think is really exciting for people to be doing work there. The biggest limitation is that you don't have these good feedback loops. That in fact we don't know what form widely deployed agents will take and how much we're talking about individually highly.

【译】……即 AI 在经济中被广泛部署的版本，这在整个技术栈上下都有影响。其中一种情形就是你之前暗示的：当至少有一些人把他们的代理设置得异常持久、异常自主地运行，并给它们非常宽泛的目标——就是赚钱、提供有用的服务。那真的是一个非常不同的世界。法律史很大程度上就是人类摸索如何应对不合作或不可预测的代理人的历史，对吧？或者限制风险，或者搞清楚我们希望事情怎样才可预测——当我跟你的公司做一笔交易，约定我们彼此合作，双方都会履行。所以从公司法到合同法再到其他很多东西，在很多方面都是我们摸索如何应对不可预测或不可信任的代理人的过程。而一旦你把 AI 加进来，就多出了一整个层级的复杂性，我认为这才是我们应该考虑的那种量级的干预。当这种事在人类之间发生时，或者当我们创造出公司之类的东西时，那需要创造全新的法律体系，不是那种在边缘修修补补的事。它更像是创造反垄断法、知识产权法或者所有这些我们已经习以为常的东西。它们并不是本来就存在的，不是当年凭空从虚空中冒出来的。它们是在特定的政治时刻、在我们不得不处理一些事情时出现的东西。而 AI 正把我们带回那个时刻。对我们中的许多人来说，我们这辈子从未经历过一个谈论创立或创造全新法律体系来处理问题的时代。所以这大概就是外围边缘。我认为在那里做工作是非常令人兴奋的。最大的局限是你没有良好的反馈循环。事实上我们不知道广泛部署的代理会是什么形态，也不知道我们谈论的多少是单个高度……

【译】……能干的系统，还是分布在世上的多智能体集群之类的东西。是的，这就成了相当棘手的条件——要在这种条件下发展出全新的法律体系。听你讲这些的时候我想到一件事：节目之友 Dean Ball 最近一直在强调，你可能也看到了，特拉华州公司法（Delaware corporate law）。按比较传统的思路，他的看法是——我不能替他发言，但我想我能转述一点——发展这些全新的法律体系听起来相当难，要花很长时间，而我们可能没有太长的时间。但我们已经有一套发展成熟的法律体系，也许适用范围比人们以为的更广，那就是特拉华州公司法，因为它规定，你作为公司的高管，负有某些义务，你不能只是放弃这些义务，把这些重大的资源分配决策——考虑到算力使用，这在很大程度上就是 AI 研发决策的本质——拱手让出。你不能就这么撒手不管，把它交给某种外星生物。当初的约定可不是这样，对吧？所以他的想法是，像 Anthropic 这样的公司现在是公开上市的，也许是件好事，因为所有这些试图确保公司高管专注于他们该专注的事、专注于他们被雇来做的事、承担起托付给他们的责任的法律，也许这真的可能非常相关。你怎么看这个论证？是的，它看起来确实相关。但我不认为它解决了问题，对吧？很大程度上因为它不是宇宙的中心，它也不是中心。特拉华州并没有获得所有这些技术专长，也没有处在许多这类 AI 政策辩论的中心去观察。

【译】……到不同的创新路径上。另一些提案——还是这个更具前瞻性的类别，谈论给单个代理绑定银行账户并在那里施加处罚——我认为设想的是一个我们确实拥有更多主权代理（sovereign agents）的世界，在那里我们可能面临不完全受个人或公司控制的代理的问题，所以我认为为那个世界准备好提案是重要的，但与此同时它们并不能解决所有问题，把责任加到公司和个人身上仍然会有它的作用。我觉得很有意思的一点是，比如在 Napster 案里，当大量的人同时违法时，法律很难执行下去，对吧？如果你面对的是几百万人，你可以杀鸡儆猴处理一两个，但你没法让几百万人都走一遍司法程序。比如在 Napster 案中，美国唱片业协会（RIAA）追了几起案子，但大量的人没有被起诉，海盗湾（Pirate Bay）至今存在，种子文件至今还在流传，对吧？所以你觉得这在多大程度上——甚至今天我就看到很多人直接把他们的钱包或 Stripe 账户分配给他们的代理，然后叫它们去赚钱，对吧？而我完全能看到这事大规模发生，几百万人、几千万人给他们的代理分配某种消费能力，叫它们去赚钱，然后这些代理去做坏事，那责任最终会落在哪里？因为再说了，你或许可以拿两三个人杀鸡儆猴，但你不可能一次性把一百万人或一千万人入罪。你认为责任最终会落在为代理提供运行能力的超大规模云（hyperscalers）身上，还是模型开发者身上，还是最终会落在哪里？是的，不幸的……

Fortunate answer is oftentimes it's not going to be clear, and it's going to take a legal process to figure out where it lies in the chain. They will figure out how to allocate responsibility in between each other, and this sort of removes the problem. If you involve users, though, you do not have complex actors who are contracting for the exact sort of who is responsible within this, and you don't get these really clear sort of priced in deterrent effects. And so, we should expect that there will be a lot of chaos from this; that there will be a lot of sort of under deterrence or under under penalizing of the behavior. It's going to leave a lot of these sort of long time lags. People are not going to be totally internalizing their cost, and that that's why I guess in addition to liability or or other forms of responsibility, we are going to need some amount of ex ante regulation that is going to be the sort of efficient place to deal with a lot of these issues rather than having everyone have to sue each other. Banned a little bit ahead of the curve. Another part is sort of what role the public plays. One role that I think has been under underplayed is going back to earlier comments. And there also there are just selection effects, ideologically or expertise wise, where you're going to end up with a more homogenous group under any admin in government. And so it's really useful to have all of the ornery smart people online arguing with each other about things that are disclosed publicly. I think part of why that has been emphasized less than many policy proposals is that going back a couple years, industry was very very antagonistic towards any proposal that involved releasing information publicly. I think that at the time policymakers were sometimes sympathetic to that. I think once they have started to see actual incidents, right? Once you have an actual, you messed up. You failed to communicate about this autonomous cyber incident. You were not fully disclosing. They have quickly updated much more towards no, no, no. When in doubt, release this information to the public. And I think that that is the the better position and one that sort of a lot of policy proposals now would be served well by including express provisions on how information is going to be revealed to the public, even if it has to be redacted, even if it has to go through some sort of filter, even if it's delayed, whatever. Figuring out what is the pathway to sharing this information publicly so that they can scrutinize it themselves and also so that some of the political incentives can sort of update, such that that people stay abreast of what's happening. Like all these countries are just very small countries, and there's just a lot. They just highly compromised. Like if you think about Jensen and what he did, and you think about like and and we were joking like you know my friends and I were joking like oh man that's like a tip of the iceberg for the South Korean or Taiwanese guy like oh my gosh, right? And I think besides the philandering, the philandering goes hand in hand with a lot of this like you know corruption, self dealing, money laundering. Like it's it's all there. It'll all be there because it's you know a lot of these countries it's not possible to do business otherwise. Like there's a there's a set of like insane requirements that you have. Like for example, they're like there's no limited liability like really most of these countries when you sign for a bank loan, you basically sign your entire life away. Like it's like equivalent to personal guarantee in the U.S. And so as a result, they launder their proceeds into family accounts so that if the main guy goes down, the family still has wealth attached to it, and it's not like the main guy goes down and everyone else like is caught up in a bank bankruptcy proceeding. So there's like a bunch of like legal structures, etc., etc., that have evolved over time in these countries in order to preserve wealth and power over time, and basically the the central party is always like super compromised, and like absolutely like can be thrown into prison and exists as a result of political stalemates that are there when the politics change.

【译】窃听泄露案被判四个月监禁。我们有个笑话，说所有韩国总统卸任后都会进监狱。所有这些人，他们全都被彻底拿捏了，如果他们不配合，比如就有东西被曝光，然后就是腐败之类的，等等等等，就被曝光。美国——美国窃听所有这些人，这些人全都被彻底拿捏了。一切都是透明的，所以只看美国想不想让这些人掌权，而那里的每一个——那里每个人都知道。他们知道自己被盯着。他们知道自己被拿捏了。他们不在乎。他们就想：好，我配合，我就没事。我不配合，如果我——如果我偏离得太远，我就有风险。所以这是个民主国家，如果你腐败、你挪用，而他们有证据——那些检察官得到另一个政党的政治保护，然后他们就上，他们就起诉，对吧？美国搞垮过三——你知道，三星会长蹲了一阵子监狱，然后美国就说：好，这个人我们可以打交道。我们把他弄出来。他在韩国被特赦放了出来。所有这些国家都只是很小的国家，而且——他们被高度拿捏。比如，你想想 Jensen 干的事，你想想——我们当时在开玩笑，我和我朋友开玩笑说，天哪，那对韩国人或台湾人来说只是冰山一角，对吧，天哪。而我认为，除了拈花惹草——拈花惹草和很多这种腐败、自我交易、洗钱是相伴相生的。全都在那儿。全都会在那儿，因为你知道，在很多这样的国家，不那样就没法做生意。有一套疯狂的要求你必须满足。比如，没有真正的有限责任，在大多数这些国家，你签银行贷款的时候，基本上是把自己的一辈子都签进去了。相当于美国的个人担保（personal guarantee）。所以结果就是，他们把收益洗进家族账户，这样万一主心骨倒台，家族还能保住与之相连的财富，而不是主心骨一倒，其他所有人都被卷进银行破产程序。所以有一堆法律结构等等等等，在这些国家随着时间演化出来，为了长时期保住财富和权力，而基本上执政党总是被超级拿捏，绝对可以被扔进监狱，它的存在是那里政治僵局的产物，而当政治变化时。

[9720s - 9840s]

Change when the U.S. decides, like, okay, I'm going to push a button here, like stuff gets exposed and someone goes to prison. But you know, prisons there are soft. You know, you don't you don't have the hard prison that you have here. Some of them have like you know day release, and four or five years later they get pardoned or you know they get like paroled and they're out. So this is yeah. I guess I'm just not so sure. I mean, we're the whole point of this exercise is to really ask: Are we entering a new era where the old rules don't apply? Right, like that's that's the core thought experiment I'm trying to engage in, and I think it seems like very plausible in this scenario if the you know if the Taiwanese public, I mean, and there's like you know, far from an expert in this, obviously as well, but like there are more pro-China factions within Taiwanese society that like aren't super thrilled with being so adversarial toward China all the time, right? I mean, isn't this like one of the central axes in Taiwanese politics? Like, are you sort of more conciliatory toward China, or are you like very hawkish? I I I I I know a lot of Taiwanese people. I know like like like literally. Doesn't that isn't that like basically the core fault line in Taiwanese politics? No, it's like it's like it's like yes, you can be you can want to make money from China, but that's different from like I want to be part of China. Yeah, and I don't think anybody's saying that. I think part of the deal that the Taiwanese would be trying to carve out for themselves in this scenario is like we're part of part of the okay. How does maybe another way to frame the question is this? Taiwan wants to be independent. I do believe that to be true. No, they don't want to be part of China. They want to be independent. They don't want to be part of China.

【译】当政治变化时，当美国决定：好，我要在这里按个按钮，就有东西被曝光，就有人进监狱。但你知道，那里的监狱很舒服。你不会有我们这里这种硬茬监狱。有些人有白天放风的待遇，四五年后他们被特赦，或者被假释出来。所以就是这样。我想我只是不太确信。我是说，这整个练习的重点就是真正地问一句：我们是不是正在进入一个旧规则不再适用的时代？对，这就是我想做的核心思想实验，而我认为在这个情景里看起来非常合理——如果台湾公众，我是说，显然我也远非专家——但台湾社会里有更亲中的派系，他们对一直这么对抗中国不太满意，对吧？我是说，这难道不是台湾政治的主轴之一吗？你要么对中国更温和一些，要么非常好战？我认——我认——我认识很多台湾人。我是说真的认识。这难道不是台湾政治的核心断层线吗？不，是——是——是的，你可以想从中国赚钱，但那和「我想成为中国的一部分」是两回事。是啊，我也没人在这么说。我认为台湾人在这个情景里想为自己争取的交易的一部分是，我们——好。也许换个方式来问这个问题。台湾想要独立。我相信这是真的。不，他们不想成为中国的一部分。他们想独立。他们不想成为中国的一部分。

[9840s - 9960s]

But that doesn't mean that they want to risk like having full independence and risks the Chinese. Well, okay, sure. De facto today, they basically operate as an independent country, right? Like, I know that there's the sort of semantic realm in which there are certain things that if they were to say, would be considered to be highly provocative to China, and so they sort of restrain themselves. Usually, when it comes to how they talk about it, they're like careful in their word choice. But like, just leaving that aside, in terms of just the practical lived reality, Taiwan operates its own government, has its own military, it has its own trade relationships, it has its own sort of special strategically ambiguous protective status that it enjoys or maybe enjoys less all the time from the United States. And I think it mostly wants to maintain that de facto independence for itself. It doesn't want to be part of China. I think that's like pretty clear from what I understand. It doesn't want to be the 51st state. It wants to kind of mostly continue to choose its own path and continue to have like elections. So what's the best way for it to maintain that? Like for the longest time, it's been cozy up to the US, do whatever the US says on all these different fronts. That secures, you know, this sort of at least like strategic ambiguity, which has been enough to keep the mainland at bay. And now I'm like, okay, maybe a different answer could be a better answer for them. Maybe a better answer is like, we're going to do business with both sides. We want to have both sides dependent on us. We have for the foreseeable future a really critical strategic resource. We don't want to be fought over because of our possession of that resource. We want to be something that nobody can touch because of our control of that resource, and so.

【译】但这不意味着他们想冒险——为了彻底独立而去冒险、去触怒中国人。嗯，好，当然。今天在事实上，他们基本上就是作为一个独立国家在运作，对吧？我知道在语义层面上，有些话他们要是说了，会被视为对中国的高度挑衅，所以他们在怎么谈论这件事上有所克制，用词很小心。但把那个放一边，就实际生活的现实而言，台湾运作着自己的政府，有自己的军队，有自己的贸易关系，有它自己那种战略模糊（strategic ambiguity）的特殊保护地位——这是它从美国那里享有的，或者也许享有的程度一直在下降。而我认为它主要想为自己保住那种事实上的独立。它不想成为中国的一部分。据我理解，这一点相当清楚。它不想成为第 51 个州。它想大体上继续选择自己的道路，继续有选举。那对它来说，维持这一点的最佳方式是什么？很长一段时间以来，都是巴结美国，在所有这些不同战线上美国说什么就做什么。这就保住了那种至少是战略模糊的地位，而这一直足以让大陆不敢轻举妄动。而现在我在想，好，也许另一个答案对他们来说是更好的答案。也许更好的

[9960s - 10080s]

We sell a little bit here, we sell a little bit there. Nobody can really afford to disrupt the status quo right now. I think we're in a world where, like, China kind of can afford to disrupt the status quo. Not that they want to go full on invasion, but like, they don't really care if TSMC capacity goes offline in today's world all that much. They might even think that's kind of convenient. I mean, I don't know. They can eat a lot of bitterness in China. I know that. No. Is the U.S. really riding to their rescue? I'm just like, I don't know. If I'm them, I may be rethinking my overall, I mean, strategy. One, one, one of the Chinese's main problems right now is that their economy is very tied to the American economy and the American consumer, because the entire economy is built around like supplying the American consumer. And so, like anything that affects the American economy and the American consumer affects them deeply, right? Um, and and their economy is not doing well because they're undergoing a Japan-style like property depression. Economies, and and I've been talking about this for I don't know like a year, and people people online have been like criticizing me, like, no, China is great, etc, etc. But news has you know filtered out over the last year, like China is doing really badly. Like people are, people don't have jobs, and a lot of the young graduates don't have jobs, and they don't. And AI is looking like the employment is high. Yeah, AI looks like it's going to wipe out like a lot of the like middle class like white collar jobs. People are scared, and the government is like stepping in to prevent tech companies from firing people. This is not a healthy economy. Like wiping, like you know, having like a minus two percent global growth from like you know attacking or like you know blocking Taiwan is not going to be a good thing for them because they're levered on the U.S. consumer. Like the U.S. will see a minus two percent, they're going to see a minus ten, right? So I don't, I don't think this whole like oh China can do whatever it likes because they haven't been willing to take the pain. They could have taken the pain with like property defaults, restructuring the financial sector. They could have done what the U.S. did post 2008, had bankruptcies, etc.

【译】答案是：我们两边都做生意。我们要两边都依赖我们。在可预见的未来，我们掌握着一种真正关键的战略资源。我们不想因为我们拥有这种资源而被人争夺。我们想成为一种因为我们对这种资源的控制而没人敢碰的存在，所以——我们这边卖一点，那边卖一点。现在谁也真的承受不起打破现状。我认为我们所在的世界是，中国勉强承受得起打破现状。倒不是说他们想全面入侵，但他们其实并不那么在乎台积电的产能在当今世界下线。他们甚至可能觉得那挺方便的。我是说，我不知道。在中国他们能吃很多苦。这我知道。不——美国真的会来救他们吗？我就是，我不知道。如果我是他们，我可能在重新思考我的整体——策略。中国人现在的主要问题之一是，他们的经济和美国经济、和美国消费者绑得很紧，因为整个经济都是围绕着给美国消费者供货建立起来的。所以任何影响美国经济和美国消费者的事，都会深深地影响他们，对吧？而且他们的经济表现不好，因为他们正在经历一场日本式的房地产萧条。经济——而我谈这个已经谈了不知道一年了，网上的人一直批评我，说：不，中国很棒等等等等。但过去一年传出来的消息，中国现在糟得很。人们——人们没有工作，很多年轻毕业生没有工作，他们没有。而 AI 看起来就业——失业率很高。是啊，AI 看起来会抹掉很多中产阶层的白领工作。人们害怕，政府正在介入阻止科技公司裁人。这不是一个健康的经济。比如，因为攻打或者封锁台湾而让全球增长掉了两个百分点，对他们来说不会是件好事，因为他们是押在美国消费者身上的。美国看到的是负 2%，他们看到的会是负 10%，对吧？所以我不认为这整套「中国想干什么就干什么」，因为他们一直不愿意承受痛苦。他们本可以承受痛苦——让房地产违约，重组金融部门。他们本可以像美国 2008 年之后那样做，有破产等等。

[10080s - 10200s]

They decided not to. They decided to go the Japan route, which is, you know, take the least like actual upfront pain and take the slow burn and the slow decline over like the you know last ten years. Like they're not they're they're not these like impervious you know impervious beings you know they they yeah yeah I think that's it is a good I think it's a good corrective to keep in mind that there are a lot of constraints on Chinese leadership decisions as well that they certainly do not have total freedom of action. Um, I felt that when I went there this summer again, obviously short stay, you know, limited expertise. But one of the impressions I had coming back was they are not going to be quick to launch a war because they, if nothing else, like every young dude that would get sent to that war is the only grandchild of, in many cases, four living grandparents, and you know, there's nothing that's going to piss people off more than their entire family tree, which is already this like upside down pyramid coming to an end over like what exactly. So I do think that it is it is good to keep in mind that they are also like much more constrained than it may appear from afar. But I don't know. I think the the same time I do feel like our hegemony may be in the process of being shown to be much more of a paper tiger than we like to think, and then you know others have kind of worked from the assumption you know for a long time, and it really isn't clear to me. Still, this has been a good discussion, but I it still isn't really that clear to me that the U.S. can dictate terms to Taiwan if it decides it wants to be a little more neutral.

【译】他们决定不那么做。他们决定走日本路线，也就是承受最少的、实实在在的前期痛苦，接受慢燃（slow burn）和过去这十年的缓慢衰退。他们不是那种刀枪不入的存在，他们——是啊，是啊，我认为这是个很好的——是个很好的提醒，要记住中国领导层的决策也受到很多约束，他们当然没有完全的行动自由。我今年夏天去那儿的时候有这种感觉——显然待得短，专业知识有限。但我回来时的印象之一是，他们不会轻易发动战争，因为，且不说别的，每一个会被送上战场的年轻小伙子，在很多情况下是四位在世祖父母唯一的孙辈，你知道，没有什么比这更让人愤怒的了——他们整个家族之树本来就已经是个倒金字塔，结果就为了……到底为了什么，就断了。所以我确实认为，记住这一点是好的：他们受约束的程度也远比远处看起来的要大。但我不知道。我认为与此同时，我确实觉得我们的霸权也许正在被证明更像是一只纸老虎（paper tiger），比我们自己愿意以为的要甚，而其他人很长时间以来一直是从那个假设出发在行动，而我真的不清楚。尽管如此，这是一场很好的讨论，但我仍然不太清楚美国能不能对台湾规定条件——如果它决定想更中立一点的话。

[10200s - 10320s]

Because, yeah, if we were like really mobilized as a society, and you know, everybody was bought in in a post Pearl Harbor sort of way, then yeah, we might be able to. But we're a long, you know, what we actually have is like anti-data center, anti-data center sentiment run rampant, and the idea that we've got to go like, you know, mobilized or like devote some extreme resources or take some extreme risks because otherwise Taiwan's going to sell chips to China. Like, I just don't think that's going to resonate with the American public at all. And so I think they actually probably have a lot more freedom of action than they realize in that sense, because our ability to like really escalate and dictate terms is waning. I, as far as I can tell, which doesn't necessarily make me happy. I mean, I maybe think it could be better if we still had all that power, but like, maybe, maybe, maybe not. But I'm just trying to see the world accurately, and I, I have a hard time telling a story that feel that rings true to me, where it's like the State Department, you know, gets to decide everything that happens, and and these other entities like don't have much of a say. Feels to me like we're in a fair, a much more like messy, you know, many polar world than we may have reckoned with. It's. I would say it's not that the other entities don't have a say, but it's like you have factions inside the Taiwanese government, you have factions inside the State Department, you have one faction in the State Department allied with one faction in the Taiwanese government, you have another faction in the Taiwanese government allied with another part of the State Department. So you have these like two two entities which are like basically talking to each other. So it's not like it's not like us against the Taiwanese government. It's like one part of the State Department with one part of the Taiwanese like.

【译】因为，是啊，如果我们作为社会真的动员起来，每个人都以一种珍珠港之后的方式认同投入，那么是的，我们也许能做到。但我们差得远，我们实际拥有的是反数据中心情绪到处蔓延，然后这种想法——我们得动员起来，或者投入极端资源、承担极端风险，否则台湾就要卖芯片给中国了——我就是不认为这会在美国公众那里引起任何共鸣。所以我认为在那个意义上，他们实际的行动自由可能比他们自己意识到的要大得多，因为我们真正升级、规定条件的能力正在减弱。据我看是这样，这不一定让我高兴。我是说，我也许觉得如果我们还拥有所有那些力量会更好，但也许——也许好，也许不好。我只是想准确地看清世界，而我很难编出一个我自己觉得站得住的故事，说国务院决定了发生的一切，而其他这些实体没有多少发言权。我感觉我们所在的是一个比我们也许设想的要混乱得多的多极（many polar）世界。这——我会说，不是其他实体没有发言权，而是台湾政府内部有派系，国务院内部有派系，国务院里的一个派系和台湾政府里的一个派系结盟，台湾政府里的另一个派系和国务院的另一部分结盟。所以你看到的是两个实体，基本上是在互相说话。所以不是我们对抗台湾政府。而是国务院的一部分和台湾——

[10320s - 10440s]

You know, leadership against another part of the state parliament with another part of the Taiwanese leadership, and both sides talking. It's it's just this very integrated kind of fluid, like you know, fifty year relationship, where both parties have like multiple points of contact. It's not like one guy talking to the other. It's like these fingers interlinked, and they're they're they're they're all like you know chatting with each other, and two factions together like proceeding on one on one thrust, and another two factions together are proceeding on another. So it's it's not this like it's it's not this like one relationship. You know, it's it's really this integrated, like you know, very complex, multi generational, like you know, multi sectoral, economic, political, like military. We have like joint military exercises with them for like last thirty or forty years. We have like, um, you know, they don't have enough. These countries don't have enough like airspace to train F sixteen pilots. So the F sixteen pilots are all trained in Arizona, right? So the you have like in Arizona, you have like you know U S Air Force, Taiwanese Air Force, Singaporean Air Force, Japanese Air Force, and they're all training together, and they're all using F sixteen s, and the supply chain for the F sixteen parts all stretches back into the U S. So the U S says like no, all of the supply chain for the F sixteen maintenance parts goes away, and these planes they need like you replace the entire plane every like two years, all the parts need to be replaced. So once the supply chain is gone, that's it. The plane is dead. You know, so there's all of these like linkages that it it's just not this whole like you know flip switch one on one. It's more like we are like together. We've been together for a long time, and we have to make things work as a group, as a team, right? It's not it's not a simplistic relationship. It's it's just so much more complex and so much more integrated. I think there's about eight hundred thousand Taiwanese Americans in Taiwan. If you go to the school in the U S. and you're planning to settle in the U S. you can escape Taiwanese national service.

【译】领导层的一部分对抗「州议会」（state parliament）的另一部分和台湾领导层的另一部分，两边在对话。这是一种非常一体化、流动的关系，一种五十年之久的关系，双方有多个接触点。不是一个人和另一个人对话。而是这些手指交叉扣在一起，他们全都在互相聊，两个派系一起推进一条路线，另外两个派系一起推进另一条路线。所以它不是那种——它不是一种单一关系。它真的是这种一体化的、非常复杂的、跨世代的、跨行业的、经济的、政治的、军事的关系。我们和他们联合军演已经有三四十年了。我们有——你知道，他们没有——这些国家没有足够的空域来训练 F-16 飞行员。所以 F-16 飞行员全都在亚利桑那州受训，对吧？所以在亚利桑那，你有美国空军、台湾空军、新加坡空军、日本航空自卫队，他们一起训练，都用 F-16，而 F-16 零件的供应链全都延伸回美国。所以美国一说「不」，F-16 维修零件的全部供应链就没了，而这些飞机——你每隔大约两年就要把整架飞机的所有零件都换一遍。所以供应链一断，就完了。飞机就死了。你知道，所以有所有这些联动，它不是那种按一下开关的一对一关系。更像是我们在一起。我们在一起很久了，我们得作为一个集体、一个团队把事情搞定，对吧？这不是一种简单化的关系。它要复杂得多、一体化得多。我想在台湾大约有八十万台裔美国人。如果你去美国上学，并且打算定居美国，你可以免服台湾兵役。
